Case: Seemant Kumar Singh vs. Mahesh PS & Ors.
Date: 21st March 2023

---

Q: What evidence did the prosecution present against Naresh Chaubey?
A: The prosecution presented evidence from 22 witnesses and several documents, proving that Naresh Chaubey processed and recommended payment of forged bills not brought through the proper channel.
Case: Naresh Chaubey vs. Central Bureau of Investigation
Date: 16th November 2017

---

Q: What was the nature of the injuries found on the victim in this case?
A: The victim had multiple injuries including scratches, contusions, and abrasions on various parts of her body, and evidence of severe sexual assault, including injuries to her genital and anal areas.
Case: Bhagwani vs. The State of Madhya Pradesh
Date: 18th January 2022

---

Q: What evidence did the prosecution present to establish the involvement of Jagdish and Prakash in the crime?
A: The prosecution presented evidence including the identification of Jagdish and Prakash by Shiv Bhagwan, blood-stained clothes found in the vehicle, and the recovery of Ram Chandra's dead body based on their disclosure statements.
Case: Jagdish & Ors. vs. The State of Rajasthan
Date: 22nd February 2023

---

Q: What role did PW1 (Manoj Satote) and PW8 (Vimalabai) play in the case?
A: PW1 (Manoj Satote) was the complainant and an eyewitness, while PW8 (Vimalabai) was also an eyewitness and a victim who survived the assault.
Case: Ankush Maruti Shinde and Others vs. State of Maharashtra
Date: 5th March 2019

---

Q: On what grounds did the Additional Sessions Judge acquit Sandeep alias Kala?
A: The Additional Sessions Judge acquitted Sandeep alias Kala based on the oral testimony of witnesses and documentary medical evidence that corroborated his alibi of being admitted in a government hospital from 12th February 2005 to 16th February 2005.
Case: Sandeep alias Kala vs. State of Haryana
Date: 16th August 2022

---

Q: On what basis did the trial court acquit N. Vijayakumar initially?
A: The trial court acquitted N. Vijayakumar on the basis of material contradictions in the testimonies of key witnesses and the absence of proof beyond reasonable doubt regarding the demand and acceptance of the bribe.
Case: N. Vijayakumar vs. State of Tamil Nadu

---

Q: What were the main charges against Jatinder Kumar in the case?
A: Jatinder Kumar was charged under Sections 304-B and 498-A of the Indian Penal Code for the suicide of his wife, Meenakshi, related to dowry harassment and cruelty.
Case: Jatinder Kumar vs. State of Haryana
Date: 17th December 2019

---

Q: What was the primary evidence presented by the prosecution against Jatinder Kumar?
A: The primary evidence included the testimony of the deceased's father (PW-1), her paternal uncle (PW-2), and a relative (PW-4), who testified about the dowry demands and harassment faced by Meenakshi.
Case: Jatinder Kumar vs. State of Haryana
Date: 17th December 2019

---

Q: What argument did the defense present regarding Meenakshi's state of mind leading to her suicide?

Case: Jatinder Kumar vs. State of Haryana
Date: 17th December 2019

---

Q: What was the main legal issue in the case of Sushanta Kumar Banik vs. State of Tripura & Ors.?

Q: What was the incident that led to the charges against respondent no.2?

Q: What was the incident that led to the charges against Devidas Loka Rathod?

Q: What did the trial court conclude regarding the mental state of Devidas Loka Rathod?

Q: What injuries did the deceased suffer according to the postmortem report?

Q: What incident led to the charges against Sita Ram?

Q: What was the main incident that led to the complaint in the case of Ramji Singh & Ors. vs. The State of Uttar Pradesh?

Case: Ramji Singh & Ors. vs. The State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: Who lodged the complaint in the case and what was the relationship of the complainant with the victim?
A: Babu Ram (PW-1) lodged the complaint. He was informed about the incident by his nephew, Sarman Singh (the victim).
Case: Ramji Singh & Ors. vs. The State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: What weapons were used in the attack on Sarman Singh according to the case details?
A: Ramji Singh and Krishna Autar used guns, Laxman Singh and Lala Ram used axes, and Virendra Singh used a pitchfork.

Q: What was the primary allegation against Raj Kumar in the case?
A: The primary allegation against Raj Kumar was that he was found selling adulterated milk, with Milk Solid Non-Fat (MSNF) found to be below the prescribed standard.
Case: Raj Kumar vs The State of Uttar Pradesh
Date: 4th October 2019

---

Q: What was the sample composition found by the Public Analyst in Raj Kumar's case?
A: The Public Analyst found that the Milk Fat (MF) was 4.6% and Milk Solid Non-Fat (MSNF) was 7.7%, against the prescribed standard of 8.5%.
Case: Raj Kumar vs The State of Uttar Pradesh
Date: 4th October 2019

---

Q: Did Raj Kumar challenge the delay in analyzing the milk sample? If so, what was the court's response?
A: Yes, Raj Kumar challenged the delay in analyzing the milk sample, arguing it caused the marginal shortfall in MSNF. The court rejected this contention, stating there was no material to support it and the sample had been preserved using formalin.
Case: Raj Kumar vs The State of Uttar Pradesh
Date: 4th October 2019

---

Q: What procedural argument did Raj Kumar raise regarding Section 13(2) of the Prevention of Food Adulteration Act, 1954?
A: Raj Kumar argued that he was not given an opportunity to send his second sample to the Central Food Laboratory (CFL) for analysis. The court found this argument meritless, noting that the notice under Section 13(2) was sent, and Raj Kumar did not exercise his option to get the sample analyzed by the CFL.
Case: Raj Kumar vs The State of Uttar Pradesh
Date: 4th October 2019

---

Q: What did the courts conclude regarding Raj Kumar's claim of illiteracy and the signatures obtained by the Food Inspector?
A: The courts concluded that the signatures obtained by the Food Inspector were indeed Raj Kumar's, and this issue could not be revisited in the proceedings.
Case: Raj Kumar vs The State of Uttar Pradesh
Date: 4th October 2019

---

Q: How did the court address the argument that marginal deficiencies in milk quality should be overlooked?
A: The court rejected the argument that marginal deficiencies should be overlooked, emphasizing that even minimal deviations from the prescribed standards cannot be ignored under the law.
Case: Raj Kumar vs The State of Uttar Pradesh
Date: 4th October 2019

---

Q: What was the court's view on the need to prove that the adulterated food was injurious to health under the Prevention of Food Adulteration Act, 1954?
A: The court stated that under the Prevention of Food Adulteration Act, 1954, it is not necessary to prove that the adulterated food was injurious to health; the failure to meet the prescribed standards is sufficient to deem the food adulterated.
Case: Raj Kumar vs The State of Uttar Pradesh
Date: 4th October 2019

---

Q: What was the Court's decision regarding the acquittal of Ramesh Shetty?
A: The Court upheld the acquittal of Ramesh Shetty, agreeing with the Designated Court's decision.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Ramesh Shetty

---

Q: What clarifying statement did the Court make about the necessity of a valid sanction under TADA?
A: The Court clarified that a valid sanction under TADA is crucial for a legitimate prosecution and impacts the jurisdiction of the Designated Court.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Ramesh Shetty

---

Q: What was the predictive question raised by the Court's judgement in this case?
A: The predictive question was whether future cases would ensure strict compliance with the requirement for valid sanction under TADA.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Ramesh Shetty

---

Q: What did the Court suggest as the next steps for the prosecution following the invalidation of the sanction?
A: The Court suggested that the prosecution could seek a fresh and valid sanction to proceed with the case lawfully.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Ramesh Shetty

---

Q: Why did the Court emphasize the significance of a valid sanction in this case?
A: The Court emphasized the significance of a valid sanction because it forms the foundation for a legitimate prosecution under stringent laws like TADA.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Ramesh Shetty

---

Q: What was the main issue in the case of M/S A.K. Sarkar & Co. & Anr. vs The State of West Bengal & Ors.?

Case: M/S A.K. Sarkar & Co. & Anr. vs The State of West Bengal & Ors.

---

Q: What were the allegations against M/S A.K. Sarkar & Co. & Anr. in this case?
A: The allegations were that the appellants sold sugar boiled confectioneries that did not display the complete address of the manufacturer and the date of manufacturing, violating Rule 32(c) and (f) of the Prevention of Food Adulteration Rules, 1955.
Case: M/S A.K. Sarkar & Co. & Anr. vs The State of West Bengal & Ors.

---

Q: What was the primary legal issue in the case of Babanrao Rajaram Pund vs M/s. Samarth Builders & Developers & Anr.?
A: The primary legal issue was whether Clause 18 of the Development Agreement dated 29th May 2014 constituted a valid arbitration agreement.
Case: Babanrao Rajaram Pund vs M/s. Samarth Builders & Developers & Anr.

---

Q: What did Clause 18 of the Development Agreement stipulate?
A: Clause 18 stipulated that all disputes or differences arising between the parties would be referred to the arbitration of a Sole Arbitrator mutually appointed, failing which, two Arbitrators, one appointed by each party, would appoint a third Arbitrator.
Case: Babanrao Rajaram Pund vs M/s. Samarth Builders & Developers & Anr.

---

Q: What was the main contention of the Respondents regarding Clause 18?
A: The Respondents contended that Clause 18 lacked the essential ingredients of a valid arbitration agreement as it did not mandate that the decision of the arbitrator would be final and binding on the parties.
Case: Babanrao Rajaram Pund vs M/s. Samarth Builders & Developers & Anr.

Q: What were the allegations against Arun Kumar C.K in the case of Sumitha Pradeep vs. Arun Kumar C.K & Anr.?
A: Arun Kumar C.K was alleged to have sexually assaulted his 12-year-old niece by asking her to sit on his lap, hugging her, kissing her cheeks, attempting to kiss her on the lips, and making lewd comments.
Case: Sumitha Pradeep vs. Arun Kumar C.K & Anr.
Date: 21st October 2022

---

Q: What impact did the incident have on the victim according to the court records?
A: The incident traumatized the victim to an extent that she slumped in her academic performance, and despite counseling sessions, she initially did not disclose the incident due to fear.
Case: Sumitha Pradeep vs. Arun Kumar C.K & Anr.
Date: 21st October 2022

---

Q: What was the key evidence against Pawan Kumar Chourasia according to the prosecution?
A: The key evidence against Pawan Kumar Chourasia was his extra-judicial confession, where he admitted to killing the boys and burying their bodies, as testified by PW-7, PW-8, and PW-9.
Case: Pawan Kumar Chourasia vs. State of Bihar
Date: 14th March 2023

---

Q: What evidence did the prosecution rely on to prove the charges against M. Ramalingam?
A: The prosecution relied on the jewel loan movement register and the testimony of bank officials to establish that loans were sanctioned without actual jewels being pledged.
Case: M. Ramalingam vs. State Represented by Inspector of Police, SBE/CBI/ACB, Madras
Date: 3rd October 2019

---

Q: What was the nature of the crime committed by the appellants in the case of Birbal Choudhary @ Mukhiya Jee vs. State of Bihar?
A: The crime involved the abduction of Ajay Shanker Mishra, Manoj Singh, and Raju Mishra for extracting ransom. The victims were kidnapped and held in captivity while a ransom demand was made.
Case: Birbal Choudhary @ Mukhiya Jee vs. State of Bihar

---

Q: How did the prosecution establish the case against the appellants in the case of Birbal Choudhary @ Mukhiya Jee vs. State of Bihar?
A: The prosecution led 22 witnesses as part of its evidence, including testimonies from the victims, the informant, and various business associates. Test Identification Parades (TIPs) and the recovery of ransom money also played a significant role in establishing the case.
Case: Birbal Choudhary @ Mukhiya Jee vs. State of Bihar

---

Q: What triggered the incident that led to the charges against Ajmer Singh and others?
A: The incident was triggered when Surender Singh tried to drive his tractor-trolley through a disputed passage, leading to a confrontation and subsequent fight involving lathi and spade blows.
Case: Ajmer Singh & Ors. vs. State of Haryana
Date: 11th April 2023

---

Q: What were the injuries sustained by the complainant party during the incident?
A: The complainant party, including Rajesh Kumar, Jagdish Chand, Lajwanti, Ravi Kumar, and Sanjeev Kumar, sustained injuries from lathi and spade blows during the fight.
Case: Ajmer Singh & Ors. vs. State of Haryana
Date: 11th April 2023

---

Q: What was the appellants' defense regarding their involvement in the incident?
A: The appellants claimed that the complainant party was the aggressor, provoked by the usage of the passage, and that they acted in self-defense during a free fight involving both parties.
Case: Ajmer Singh & Ors. vs. State of Haryana
Date: 11th April 2023

---

Q: What was the State's argument regarding the subsequent developments in the case?
A: The State argued that the appeal had become purely academic because a closure report had already been filed, and the CDs containing the alleged hate speech were found to be tampered.
Case: Parvez Parwaz & Anr. vs. State of Uttar Pradesh & Ors.

---

Q: What did the forensic report state about the CDs submitted as evidence?
A: The forensic report stated that the CDs were tampered and edited, with one CD being in a broken condition and another found to be tampered with by the Central Forensic Science Laboratory (CFSL).
Case: Parvez Parwaz & Anr. vs. State of Uttar Pradesh & Ors.

---

Q: What were the charges against Gopinathan in the case?
A: Gopinathan was charged under Section 55(g) and 8(1) read with 8(2) of the Abkari Act for manufacturing arrack.
Case: Gopinathan vs. State of Kerala
Date: 22nd June 2022

---

Q: What were the circumstances under which Gopinathan was apprehended?
A: Gopinathan was apprehended by the police on 25th February 2007 while engaged in manufacturing arrack at a place called Kochethupara in Perunad village. The police seized 40 liters of arrack and 3750 liters of wash from the site.
Case: Gopinathan vs. State of Kerala
Date: 22nd June 2022

---

Q: What was the significance of the seizure conducted by the police in the case?
A: The police seized 40 liters of arrack and 3750 liters of wash, which were crucial evidence in proving Gopinathan's involvement in the illegal manufacturing of arrack.
Case: Gopinathan vs. State of Kerala
Date: 22nd June 2022

---

Q: What was the CBI's stance on the seized currency notes?
A: The CBI maintained that the seized currency notes, which were in the denomination of Rs. 2000, were part of the proceeds of crime and were not accounted for properly.
Case: J. Sekar @ Sekar Reddy vs. Directorate of Enforcement

---

Q: How did the court view the evidence presented by the Enforcement Directorate?
A: The court found that the Enforcement Directorate did not present sufficient evidence to prove the allegations of money laundering beyond a reasonable doubt. The court noted that the reasonable belief of the Deputy Director was based on speculation and not on concrete evidence.
Case: J. Sekar @ Sekar Reddy vs. Directorate of Enforcement

---

Q: What injuries were found on the deceased Hariram as per the injury report?
A: The injury report on Hariram showed an abrasion on the right side of the forehead, an incised wound on the right side of the neck, and a grievous incised wound on the abdomen with intestines protruding.
Case: Jodhraj & Anr. vs. State of Rajasthan

---

Q: On what grounds did the Director General of Prisons deny Atbir’s request for furlough?
A: The Director General of Prisons denied Atbir’s request for furlough based on the Hon’ble President’s order that Atbir would remain in prison for the remainder of his natural life without parole and without remission. Additionally, Atbir had not earned the last three Annual Good Conduct Reports.
Case: Atbir vs. State of NCT of Delhi

---

Q: What are the eligibility criteria for furlough under the Delhi Prison Rules, 2018?
A: Under the Delhi Prison Rules, 2018, a prisoner must have good conduct in prison, should have earned rewards in the last three Annual Good Conduct Reports, and must continue to maintain good conduct. The prisoner should also not be a habitual offender and must be a citizen of India.
Case: Atbir vs. State of NCT of Delhi

---

Q: What role do the Delhi Prison Rules, 2018 play in determining a prisoner’s eligibility for furlough?
A: The Delhi Prison Rules, 2018 provide the framework and criteria for determining a prisoner’s eligibility for furlough. These rules emphasize good conduct in prison and stipulate that furlough can be granted as a reward for maintaining such conduct, thereby encouraging prisoners to reform.
Case: Atbir vs. State of NCT of Delhi

---

Q: What led to the altercation between Nirmala Devi and the deceased, Mast Ram?
A: The altercation occurred when Mast Ram refused to give Rs. 500 to his daughter Priyanka for attending a National Cadet Corps (NCC) camp. This refusal led to a quarrel between Nirmala Devi and Mast Ram, during which Nirmala Devi inflicted blows on Mast Ram with a stick.
Case: Nirmala Devi vs. State of Himachal Pradesh

---

Q: What was the relationship between Nirmala Devi and the deceased?
A: Nirmala Devi was the wife of the deceased, Mast Ram. Their relationship was strained, and they often quarreled. They were living separately at the time of the incident.
Case: Nirmala Devi vs. State of Himachal Pradesh

---

Q: What did the post-mortem report indicate about the cause of Mast Ram's death?
A: The post-mortem report indicated that Mast Ram died due to hemorrhagic shock and oedema of the brain caused by the injuries inflicted on him.
Case: Nirmala Devi vs. State of Himachal Pradesh

---

Q: What was the incident that led to the filing of the case against the respondents?

Case: State of Rajasthan vs. Mahesh Kumar @ Mahesh Dhaulpuria & Anr.

Q: What is the case name and judgement date of the case involving Siju Kurian?
A: The case name is 'Siju Kurian vs. State of Karnataka' and the judgement date is 17th April 2023.
Case: Siju Kurian vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What was the primary evidence against Siju Kurian?
A: The primary evidence against Siju Kurian was his confession and the recovery of the deceased's body and stolen items based on his statement.
Case: Siju Kurian vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What was the role of PW-10 in the investigation?
A: PW-10 acted as an interpreter during Siju Kurian's confession, translating the statements from Malayalam to Kannada.
Case: Siju Kurian vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What were the main contentions raised by Siju Kurian's defense counsel?
A: The defense argued that there was no direct evidence, the confession was not reliable, and the prosecution failed to establish the chain of events conclusively.
Case: Siju Kurian vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What were the circumstantial evidences that pointed to Siju Kurian's guilt?
A: The circumstantial evidence included the last seen theory, recovery of the deceased's body and stolen items, and the conduct of Siju Kurian after the crime.
Case: Siju Kurian vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: Who were the key witnesses in the case against Siju Kurian?
A: The key witnesses included PW-10 (the interpreter), PW-1, PW-2, PW-4, PW-9, and PW-11, among others, who testified about various aspects of the crime and the recovery of evidence.
Case: Siju Kurian vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What was the defense argument presented by Murugan?
A: Murugan's defense argued that the evidence was circumstantial and did not conclusively prove his involvement in the crime.
Case: Murugan vs. State of Tamil Nadu

---

Q: How did the court view the testimonies of Omveer (PW-1) and Inder (PW-2)?
A: The court found that Omveer (PW-1) could not have witnessed the incident as he was inside his house during the occurrence. Inder (PW-2), although an injured witness, had inconsistencies in his testimony regarding the timing of his injuries and medical examination.
Case: Khema @ Khem Chandra Etc. vs. State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: What were the discrepancies in the medical examination of Inder (PW-2)?
A: There were discrepancies regarding the time of Inder's (PW-2) injuries and his medical examination. The injury report stated 10:20 pm, but Inder claimed he was examined during the day, leading to inconsistencies in the evidence.
Case: Khema @ Khem Chandra Etc. vs. State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: Why did the court find the recoveries of the weapons used in the crime unreliable?
A: The recoveries were found unreliable because the seizure memos were not signed by independent witnesses, and the memorandum statements under Section 27 of the Evidence Act were not placed on record.
Case: Khema @ Khem Chandra Etc. vs. State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: What principle did the court emphasize regarding the re-appreciation of evidence?
A: The court emphasized that it would re-appreciate evidence if the appreciation of evidence and findings were vitiated by errors of law or procedure, or where the conclusions were manifestly perverse, as in this case.
Case: Khema @ Khem Chandra Etc. vs. State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: What was the prosecution's case against Sakharam?
A: The prosecution argued that Sakharam and others attacked Ramnath and his family, causing serious injuries with lathis and Ballam, leading to the death of Badri and injuries to Santu and Ramnath.
Case: Sakharam vs. State of Madhya Pradesh

---

Q: What was Sakharam's defense in the appeal?
A: Sakharam's defense argued that the incident was a sudden quarrel without prior intention, and the injuries inflicted on Santu were not grievous as opined by Dr. Moitra (PW-15).
Case: Sakharam vs. State of Madhya Pradesh

---

Q: On what basis were the appellants convicted in the 'Munikrishna @ Krishna etc. vs State by Ulsoor PS' case?

Case: Munikrishna @ Krishna etc. vs State by Ulsoor PS

---

Q: What was the main injury that caused the death of S. Ramakrishnan?
A: The main injury was a 13 cm x 5 cm deep incised wound on the front of the neck, which cut the jugular veins on both sides, leading to death due to shock and hemorrhage.
Case: Munikrishna @ Krishna etc. vs State by Ulsoor PS

---

Q: What did the post-mortem report indicate about the injuries sustained by S. Ramakrishnan?
A: The post-mortem report indicated that there were seven ante mortem injuries, with the most significant being the incised wound on the neck, which caused excessive loss of blood and immediate death.
Case: Munikrishna @ Krishna etc. vs State by Ulsoor PS

---

Q: What did the inquest report state about the condition of S. Ramakrishnan's body?
A: The inquest report stated that S. Ramakrishnan's body was found in a pool of blood in the kitchen, with a bleeding injury on the neck caused by a sharp weapon, and the cupboards in the living room were open with Rs. 3000 missing.
Case: Munikrishna @ Krishna etc. vs State by Ulsoor PS

---

Q: What procedural error was argued by the appellants regarding the filing of the chargesheet in Judgebir Singh @ Jasbir Singh vs. National Investigation Agency?
A: The appellants argued that the chargesheet was erroneously filed in the Court of Magistrate instead of the Special Court notified under Section 22 of the NIA Act, which rendered all subsequent proceedings without jurisdiction and hence, a nullity.
Case: Judgebir Singh @ Jasbir Singh vs. National Investigation Agency

---

Q: What evidence was primarily used to convict the appellants in the case of Parvat Singh & Ors. vs. State of Madhya Pradesh?
A: The primary evidence used to convict the appellants was the testimony of the sole eyewitness, Mullo Bai, who was the mother of the deceased.
Case: Parvat Singh & Ors. vs. State of Madhya Pradesh

---

Q: What was the impact of the testimony of other witnesses in the case of Parvat Singh & Ors. vs. State of Madhya Pradesh?
A: The testimony of other witnesses did not support the prosecution's case, and some were declared hostile. This further weakened the prosecution's case, relying solely on the contradictory and improved testimony of the sole eyewitness.
Case: Parvat Singh & Ors. vs. State of Madhya Pradesh

---

Q: What was the context of the accident involved in the case of Mathew Alexander vs. Mohammed Shafi and Anr.?
A: The accident occurred when the Appellant's son was driving a Maruti Alto car with friends, and it collided with a gas tanker lorry driven by Ramar. The collision resulted in the deaths of the Appellant's son and five others.
Case: Mathew Alexander vs. Mohammed Shafi and Anr.

---

Q: What did the court conclude about the possibility of reforming Dattatraya @ Datta Ambo Rokade?

Case: Dattatraya @ Datta Ambo Rokade vs. State of Maharashtra

Q: What defense did Rakesh Tiwari present in response to the contempt charges?

Q: What was the main issue in the case of M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.?
A: The main issue was whether the prosecution based on the second statutory notice after the dishonor of cheques is permissible under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act.
Case: M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.

---

Q: What were the cheques in question in the case of M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.?
A: The cheques in question were three cheques issued by the respondents: Cheque No. 316693 dated 20.07.2009 for Rs.1,44,362, Cheque No. 316663 dated 30.07.2009 for Rs.4,26,400, and Cheque No. 316692 dated 10.08.2009 for Rs.4,48,656.
Case: M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.

---

Q: Why were the cheques issued by the respondents dishonored in the case of M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.?
A: The cheques were dishonored and returned with the endorsement 'insufficient funds'.
Case: M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.

---

Q: What did the appellant do after the cheques were dishonored in the case of M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.?
A: The appellant issued a statutory notice to the respondents on 25.01.2010 demanding the repayment of the amount. When the cheque amount was not paid, the appellant filed a complaint under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act.
Case: M/S. Sicagen India Ltd. vs. Mahindra Vadinei & Ors.

---

Q: What was the main incident leading to the case of Major Singh vs. State of Punjab & Anr.?
A: The incident occurred on 21st July 1998 when Major Singh assaulted his uncle, Makhan Singh, with a wooden 'Bahi' (wooden side of a cot frame) following a quarrel that took place the previous day.
Case: Major Singh vs. State of Punjab & Anr.

---

Q: What did the post-mortem report reveal about the injuries sustained by Makhan Singh?
A: The post-mortem report revealed that Makhan Singh sustained eight injuries, including a fatal head injury that led to his death due to shock and hemorrhage.
Case: Major Singh vs. State of Punjab & Anr.

---

Q: What evidence did the prosecution rely on to prove Major Singh's guilt?
A: The prosecution relied on eyewitness testimonies, the recovery of the blood-stained 'Bahi' based on Major Singh's confession, and the post-mortem report indicating the cause of death.
Case: Major Singh vs. State of Punjab & Anr.

---

Q: What was the main issue in the case of M/s Gimpex Private Limited vs. Manoj Goel?
A: The main issue was whether parallel prosecutions under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act (NI Act) for dishonour of cheques could be sustained, specifically in relation to cheques issued before and after a settlement agreement.
Case: M/s Gimpex Private Limited vs. Manoj Goel

---

Q: What was the outcome of the initial complaints filed by M/s Gimpex Private Limited under Section 138 of the NI Act?
A: The initial complaints were pending when a settlement agreement was reached. However, the cheques issued pursuant to the settlement agreement were also dishonoured, leading to a second set of complaints.
Case: M/s Gimpex Private Limited vs. Manoj Goel

---

Q: What were the terms of the settlement agreement between M/s Gimpex Private Limited and ACL?
A: The settlement agreement required ACL to pay Rs. 3 crores immediately and the remaining Rs. 7 crores in three monthly instalments. Post-dated cheques were issued for these payments, and any default would allow Gimpex to file fresh complaints under the NI Act.
Case: M/s Gimpex Private Limited vs. Manoj Goel

---

Q: What was the outcome of the initial complaint filed by Rajlakshmi Chaudhuri?
A: The initial complaint was dismissed by the learned Chief Judicial Magistrate (CJM) on 12th November 2020, citing lack of evidence and delay in filing the complaint.
Case: Kailash Vijayvargiya vs. Rajlakshmi Chaudhuri & Others

Q: What were the charges against Mohammed Akram in this case?

---

Q: What was the CBI's argument against the transfer of the cases?
A: The CBI argued that the charges framed against Devendra Kumar Saxena were serious, involving multiple counts of corruption and conspiracy. They also mentioned that a significant number of witnesses had not yet been examined in the cases.
Case: Devendra Kumar Saxena vs. Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) & Others

---

Q: What was the reason behind the issuance of the cheques by the appellants to the second respondent?
A: The cheques were issued as security for payment under a Power Supply Agreement (PSA) between the appellants' company, R.L. Steels & Energy Limited, and the second respondent for providing uninterrupted power supply at the company's plant.
Case: Sunil Todi & Ors. vs. State of Gujarat & Anr.

---

Q: What was the significance of the Power Supply Agreement (PSA) in this case?
A: The PSA was significant as it outlined the terms of payment and security for the power supply provided by the second respondent to the appellants' company, R.L. Steels & Energy Limited. The PSA required payments to be made through Letters of Credit, and the cheques issued were initially intended as security for these payments.
Case: Sunil Todi & Ors. vs. State of Gujarat & Anr.

Q: What were the charges against Ebha Arjun Jadeja and the other appellants in this case?

Q: What evidence was presented by the prosecution in 'Suresh Yadav @ Guddu vs. The State of Chhattisgarh'?

Q: What was the trial court's reason for acquitting Raveen Kumar in the case?
A: The trial court acquitted Raveen Kumar, citing reasonable doubt regarding the possession of the prohibited substance, largely due to the testimony of an independent witness who was declared hostile and contradictions in the statements of the police witnesses.
Case: Raveen Kumar vs. State of Himachal Pradesh
Date: 26th October 2020

Q: What were the specific allegations against Shri Subir Bose in the case?

Q: What was the main legal issue in the case of 'M/s. Cheminova India Ltd. & Anr. vs. State of Punjab & Anr.'?
A: The main issue was whether the complaint filed by the Insecticide Inspector against the appellants for misbranding under the Insecticides Act, 1968, was barred by limitation and whether the correct procedure was followed.
Case: M/s. Cheminova India Ltd. & Anr. vs. State of Punjab & Anr.
Date: 4th August 2021

---

Q: What were the circumstances leading to the complaint against M/s. Cheminova India Ltd. & Anr.?
A: On 10th February 2011, the Insecticide Inspector inspected the premises of M/s. Navneet Singh and found 60 tins of insecticide (Trizophos 40% E.C.). The samples tested contained 34.70% active ingredient against the labelled 40%, leading to allegations of misbranding.
Case: M/s. Cheminova India Ltd. & Anr. vs. State of Punjab & Anr.
Date: 4th August 2021

---

Q: What were the circumstances of the crime according to the prosecution in 'Kirpal Singh vs. State of Punjab'?
A: On the night of 12th/13th November 1997, Balwinder Singh was sleeping in the chaubara of his house when Kirpal Singh and another assailant attacked him with a spade, resulting in severe head injuries. Sharan Kaur, who was sleeping downstairs, was also attacked by Kirpal Singh with a knife when she opened the door upon hearing a noise.
Case: Kirpal Singh vs. State of Punjab
Date: 18th April 2024

---

Q: What was the basis of Amarjeet Singh's plea of unsoundness of mind in 'Lakhan Singh vs. Amarjeet Singh & Anr.'?

Q: What was the primary issue in the case of Government of Andhra Pradesh vs. Appellant?
A: The primary issue was the legality of the preventive detention order issued against the appellant under the Andhra Pradesh Prevention of Dangerous Activities Act, 1986.
Case: Government of Andhra Pradesh vs. Appellant

---

Q: On what grounds was the appellant detained in this case?
A: The appellant was detained on the grounds of being a 'goonda' and posing a threat to public order under the Andhra Pradesh Prevention of Dangerous Activities Act, 1986.
Case: Government of Andhra Pradesh vs. Appellant

---

Q: What was the appellant's argument against the detention order?
A: The appellant argued that the detention order was illegal as it was issued for a period of 12 months at a stretch without periodic review, contrary to the provisions of the Andhra Pradesh Prevention of Dangerous Activities Act, 1986.
Case: Government of Andhra Pradesh vs. Appellant

Q: What incident led to the disciplinary action against R. Karthik?

Case: Union of India & Ors. vs. R. Karthik
Date: 21st January 2020

---

Q: What was the primary issue in the case of 'Sunil Kumar vs. State of U.P. & Anr.'?
A: The primary issue was whether the proceedings against Sunil Kumar, initiated based on a complaint by a co-worker, should be quashed due to the trivial nature of the allegations and the lack of progress in the case since 2015.
Case: Sunil Kumar vs. State of U.P. & Anr.
Date: 3rd August 2023

---

Q: What were the allegations made by the respondent no. 2 against Sunil Kumar?
A: The respondent no. 2 alleged that Sunil Kumar had assaulted, abused, and threatened to kill him during the altercation on 11th September 2014.
Case: Sunil Kumar vs. State of U.P. & Anr.
Date: 3rd August 2023

---

Q: Why did the appellant seek to quash the chargesheet and proceedings?
A: Sunil Kumar sought to quash the chargesheet and proceedings on the grounds that the allegations were trivial and the case had been pending without progress since 2015, causing undue harassment.
Case: Sunil Kumar vs. State of U.P. & Anr.
Date: 3rd August 2023

---

Q: On what grounds did the Directorate of Enforcement freeze the appellant's bank accounts?
A: The Directorate of Enforcement froze the appellant's bank accounts to prevent the layering and diversion of proceeds of crime related to a predicate offense investigated by the Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI).
Case: OPTO Circuit India Ltd. vs. Axis Bank & Ors.
Date: 3rd February 2021

---

Q: What were the key points mentioned in the affidavit filed by the complainant?
A: The complainant mentioned that the incident happened due to a misunderstanding and in the spur of the moment. She stated that with the passage of time, relations between her and the appellants had become cordial, and they were now closely related. She expressed that she did not desire the appellants to undergo further imprisonment.
Case: Mallanna & Ors. vs. The State of Karnataka & Anr.

---

Q: What incident led to the filing of charges against Wasif Haider and the other respondents?
A: The charges were filed following a riot on 16th March 2001, during which rioters caused destruction at the Chaubey Gola Temple, and ADM Chandra Prakash Pathak was fatally shot along with his orderly, Ram Chandra, when police attempted to control the situation.
Case: State of Uttar Pradesh vs. Wasif Haider & Ors.
Date: 10th December 2018

---

Q: What are the main objectives of the Black Money (Undisclosed Foreign Income and Assets) and Imposition of Tax Act, 2015?
A: The main objectives of the Black Money Act are to unearth black money stashed in foreign countries, prevent unaccounted money from going abroad, punish individuals indulging in illegitimate means of generating money causing loss to the revenue, and prevent illegitimate income and assets from being utilized in ways detrimental to India's social, economic, and strategic interests and national security.
Case: Union of India & Ors. vs. Gautam Khaitan

---

Q: What was the prosecution's case against Sathish Kumar?
A: The prosecution's case was based on circumstantial evidence, including the timeline of calls between the appellant and his jogging partner, the suspicious behavior of the appellant, and the recovery of bloodstained shoes from his residence.
Case: Sathish Kumar A @ Sathishkumar Anand @ Sathish Kumar Gupta vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What did the postmortem report reveal about the cause of Priyanka Gupta's death?
A: The postmortem report revealed that Priyanka Gupta died due to asphyxia as a result of strangulation, despite having several incised wounds on her neck.
Case: Sathish Kumar A @ Sathishkumar Anand @ Sathish Kumar Gupta vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What were some key pieces of evidence against Sathish Kumar?
A: Key pieces of evidence included the call records showing suspicious timings, the appellant wearing black leather shoes instead of jogging shoes, and the recovery of sports shoes with bloodstains from the appellant's residence.
Case: Sathish Kumar A @ Sathishkumar Anand @ Sathish Kumar Gupta vs. State of Karnataka

---

Q: What evidence did the prosecution present to prove the charges against Dhirendra Singh?
A: The prosecution presented testimonies from eyewitnesses, including Suraj Mandal (P.W.27), Md. Akhtar Hussain (P.W.7), and Nirmal Bhattacharya (P.W.8), as well as medical evidence from the doctor who treated Suraj Mandal.
Case: Dhirendra Singh @ Pappu vs. State of Jharkhand

---

Q: What was the sequence of events leading to the filing of the complaint by PW-1 in the case?
A: On 3rd November 2002, Master Hrithik went missing. Despite searches, he was not found, leading PW-1 to file a missing complaint at PS Vidyanagar, Hubli. On 14th November 2002, Kalinga @ Kushal, in a drunken state, mentioned the incident. The next day, he confessed to PW-1, who then took him to the police station, leading to the filing of the complaint.
Case: Kalinga @ Kushal vs. State of Karnataka By Police Inspector Hubli

Q: What evidence did the prosecution rely on to convict the appellants?
A: The prosecution relied on the testimony of PW-19, who witnessed the incident, the statement of PW-20, who was informed by PW-19, and the recovery of the body and postmortem report indicating the deceased was intoxicated.

---

Q: What were the charges against Lalichan in the case 'Lalichan vs. State of Kerala'?

Case: Lalichan vs. State of Kerala

---

Q: What was the decision of the Trial Court regarding Lalichan?
A: The Trial Court convicted Lalichan under Sections 8(2) and 63 of the Kerala Abkari Act, sentencing him to one year of simple imprisonment and a fine of Rs. 1,00,000 under Section 8(2) and a fine of Rs. 5,000 under Section 63.
Case: Lalichan vs. State of Kerala

---

Q: What was the prosecution's case against the appellants?
A: The prosecution's case was that 26 persons, including the appellants, armed with deadly weapons, trespassed into the house of Md. Aziz Ali, Md. Kutub Ali, Md. Mamud Ali, and Samir Ali, dragged them outside, and assaulted them, resulting in their deaths. Another individual, Md. Atar Ali, was injured in the incident.
Case: Md. Rojali Ali & Ors. vs. The State of Assam

---

Q: What was the defense's argument regarding the credibility of the eyewitnesses?
A: The defense argued that the eyewitnesses were closely related to the deceased and had a motive to falsely implicate the appellants. They also contended that there were inconsistencies in the eyewitnesses' testimonies and that no weapons were recovered from the appellants.
Case: Md. Rojali Ali & Ors. vs. The State of Assam

Q: What was the decision of the Trial Court in 'Rajesh Jain vs. Ajay Singh'?
A: The Trial Court acquitted Ajay Singh of the charge under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881, stating that the complainant failed to prove the existence of a legally enforceable debt.
Case: Rajesh Jain vs. Ajay Singh

---

Q: What was Rajesh Jain's contention regarding the cheque issued by Ajay Singh?
A: Rajesh Jain contended that Ajay Singh had issued a post-dated cheque for Rs. 6,95,204 towards part repayment of an outstanding debt, which was dishonored due to insufficient funds.
Case: Rajesh Jain vs. Ajay Singh

---

Q: What did Ajay Singh claim in his defense against the allegations?
A: Ajay Singh claimed that he had not issued the cheque in question, asserting that it was misused by the complainant, who obtained it through his sister-in-law, Gita Sunar. He admitted to borrowing money but contested the debt amount claimed by the complainant.
Case: Rajesh Jain vs. Ajay Singh

---

Q: Who were the parties involved in the case?

---

Q: What were the findings of the post-mortem report on Juhi Arshi?
A: The post-mortem report, conducted after the body was exhumed, found the body in an advanced stage of decomposition, making it difficult to ascertain the cause of death. However, injury marks were observed on her neck, hands, and legs.

Q: What was the main issue in the case 'State of Karnataka vs. T. Naseer & Ors.'?
A: The main issue was whether the prosecution could recall a witness and produce a certificate under Section 65B of the Evidence Act after a delay, in connection with the serial bomb blasts that occurred in Bangalore on 25th July 2008.
Case: State of Karnataka vs. T. Naseer & Ors.

---

Q: What was the prosecution seeking to achieve by recalling the witness M. Krishna (PW-189)?
A: The prosecution sought to recall M. Krishna (PW-189) to produce a certificate under Section 65B of the Evidence Act to validate the electronic evidence seized in connection with the bomb blasts.
Case: State of Karnataka vs. T. Naseer & Ors.

---

Q: Why did the trial court reject the prosecution's application to recall the witness?
A: The trial court rejected the application on the grounds of delay, stating that the certificate under Section 65B of the Evidence Act was sought to be produced six years after the initial evidence submission.
Case: State of Karnataka vs. T. Naseer & Ors.

Q: What was the Enforcement Directorate's argument regarding the computation of the remand period?
A: The Enforcement Directorate argued that the date of remand should be excluded from the computation of the 60-day period, and even if the 60-day period expired on a Sunday, it should be extended to the next working day, i.e., Monday.
Case: Enforcement Directorate vs Kapil Wadhawan & Anr.
Date: 27th March 2023

---

Q: Who were the counsels representing the parties in this case?
A: The counsels representing the parties included Mr. S. V. Raju, learned Additional Solicitor General, for the appellant (Enforcement Directorate), and Mr. Mukul Rohatgi and Mr. Kapil Sibal, learned senior counsel, for the respondents (Kapil Wadhawan & Anr.).
Case: Enforcement Directorate vs Kapil Wadhawan & Anr.

---

Q: What was the Enforcement Directorate's argument regarding the computation of the remand period?

Case: Enforcement Directorate vs Kapil Wadhawan & Anr.

Case: Enforcement Directorate vs Kapil Wadhawan & Anr.
Date: 23rd February 2021

---

Q: How did the court view the presence of the dead body in Santosh @ Bhure's apartment?
A: The court found that the presence of the dead body in Santosh @ Bhure's apartment, along with the circumstantial evidence, pointed towards his involvement in the crime. However, mere tenancy was not sufficient to prove guilt beyond a reasonable doubt without additional evidence.
Case: Santosh @ Bhure vs. State (G.N.C.T.) of Delhi

Date: 10th January 2020

Q: What were the charges against Baljinder Singh and Khushi Khan in the case of State of Punjab vs Baljinder Singh & Anr.?

---

Q: What were the allegations made by the respondent, Parimala, against Shyamlal Devda and the other appellants?
A: Parimala alleged that she was a victim of domestic violence, stating that her husband and in-laws took away her jewelry and harassed her, and she sought protection, residence, and monetary relief under the Domestic Violence Act.
Case: Shyamlal Devda and Others vs Parimala
Date: 22nd January 2020

---

Q: What is Section 27 of the Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act, 2005, and how did it apply in this case?
A: Section 27 of the Domestic Violence Act specifies that the court of Judicial Magistrate or Metropolitan Magistrate where the aggrieved person resides, carries on business, or is employed, has jurisdiction to entertain a complaint. In this case, the Bengaluru court had jurisdiction as the respondent was residing in Bengaluru.
Case: Shyamlal Devda and Others vs Parimala
Date: 22nd January 2020

---

Q: What were the charges against Sajid in the case of Sajid vs The State of Uttarakhand and Another?
A: Sajid was convicted under Sections 363 and 366 of the Indian Penal Code for kidnapping and abduction with the intent to compel a woman to marry.
Case: Sajid vs The State of Uttarakhand and Another
Date: 10th August 2018

---

Q: What are Sections 2(b) and 2(c) of the Gangsters Act?
A: Section 2(b) defines a 'gang' as a group of persons acting singly or collectively through violence, threat, intimidation, or coercion with the objective of disturbing public order or gaining undue advantage. Section 2(c) defines a 'gangster' as a member, leader, or organizer of a gang who abets, assists, or harbors any person involved in such activities.
Case: Padma Mishra vs State of Uttarakhand & Anr.
Date: 13th February 2020

---

Q: What was the main motive behind the crime according to the prosecution?
A: The main motive behind the crime was Ramanand's extra-marital affair with a married lady named Manju and his desire to marry her, which was opposed by his wife Sangeeta.
Case: Ramanand @ Nandlal Bharti vs State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: What did the post-mortem reports reveal about the cause of death of Sangeeta and her daughters?
A: The post-mortem reports revealed that Sangeeta and her daughters suffered incised wounds mostly on the head and neck region, with the burn injuries being post-mortem and the incised wounds being ante-mortem. The cause of death was shock and haemorrhage as a result of ante-mortem head injuries.
Case: Ramanand @ Nandlal Bharti vs State of Uttar Pradesh

---

Q: What was the Governor of Tamil Nadu's response to the State Cabinet's recommendation for remission?
A: The Governor of Tamil Nadu referred the State Cabinet's recommendation for remission to the President of India on 25th January 2021, rather than taking a decision himself.
Case: A.G. Perarivalan vs State, Through Superintendent of Police CBI/SIT/MMDA, Chennai, Tamil Nadu

---

Q: What role did Prakash Gupta hold in the company involved in the SEBI investigation?
A: Prakash Gupta was the director and promoter of Ideal Hotels & Industries Limited, which owns a 3-star hotel in Varanasi.
Case: Prakash Gupta vs. Securities and Exchange Board of India
Date: 23rd July 2021

---

Q: What was the initial public offering (IPO) made by Ideal Hotels & Industries Limited?
A: In 1995, Ideal Hotels & Industries Limited made an IPO inviting a subscription to 38 lakh equity shares at a par value of Rs 10 per share, aggregating to Rs 380 lakhs.
Case: Prakash Gupta vs. Securities and Exchange Board of India
Date: 23rd July 2021

---

Q: What were the main allegations against Ideal Hotels & Industries Limited in SEBI's investigation?
A: The main allegations were price rigging and insider trading in the shares of Ideal Hotels & Industries Limited, and misutilization of IPO proceeds to purchase its own shares through related entities.
Case: Prakash Gupta vs. Securities and Exchange Board of India
Date: 23rd July 2021

---

Q: What was the primary reason for the conflict between Satish Kumar and the deceased Shamsher?
A: The conflict arose due to objections by Satish Kumar and Dhajja Ram to the relations of a woman belonging to 'Dahiya Gotra' with a man of 'Ohlan Gotra', which led to threats and violence against Shamsher.
Case: Satish Kumar vs The State of Haryana

---

Q: What were the key pieces of evidence against Satish Kumar in this case?
A: The key evidence included the dying declaration of Shamsher, statements from witnesses Surender (PW-2), Bharpoor (PW-7), and Sadhu Ram (PW-8), and medical reports from Dr. Jai Mala (PW-3) and Dr. R.K. Nandal (PW-4).
Case: Satish Kumar vs The State of Haryana

---

Q: Why did the trial judge initially reject the testimonies of the child witnesses PW-3 and PW-4?
A: The trial judge rejected their testimonies because the children were unable to identify the judge and lawyers, and the judge deemed them incapable of deposing evidence.
Case: P Ramesh vs State Rep by Inspector of Police

---

Q: What were the key pieces of evidence against the appellants?
A: The key pieces of evidence against the appellants were the dying declarations made by Sher Singh to Dr. Sushma and SI Rajesh Kumar, where he identified Purshottam and Suresh as his assailants.
Case: Purshottam Chopra & Anr. v. State (Govt. of NCT Delhi)

---

Q: What was the result of the post-mortem examination of Sher Singh?
A: The post-mortem examination revealed that Sher Singh died due to shock consequent to 100% ante-mortem flame burns.
Case: Purshottam Chopra & Anr. v. State (Govt. of NCT Delhi)

---

Q: What was the prosecution's response to the defense's argument about the dying declarations?
A: The prosecution maintained that both dying declarations were credible as they were recorded independently by Dr. Sushma and SI Rajesh Kumar shortly after the incident, and Sher Singh was found to be mentally fit to make the statements despite his critical condition.
Case: Purshottam Chopra & Anr. v. State (Govt. of NCT Delhi)

---

Q: What was the defense argument regarding Raju Manjhi's involvement?
A: The defense argued that Raju Manjhi was falsely implicated due to enmity, there was no concrete proof of his participation, and the recovery of Rs. 400 from his possession was not sufficient to prove his involvement in the crime.
Case: Raju Manjhi vs State of Bihar

---

Q: What was the complainant's argument against transferring the case to Allahabad?
A: The complainant argued that the alleged incidents occurred in New Delhi, and most of the witnesses, including 12 official witnesses, were based in Delhi. Transferring the case to Allahabad would hinder their official duties and cause inconvenience.
Case: Swaati Nirkhi & Ors. vs State (NCT of Delhi) & Ors.

---

Q: What was the position of the respondents regarding the publication of the documents in 'The Hindu' newspaper?
A: The respondents did not seriously dispute or contest the fact that the three documents had been published in 'The Hindu' newspaper and were thus available in the public domain.
Case: Yashwant Sinha & Ors. vs. Central Bureau of Investigation
Date: 10th April 2019

---

Q: How did the court view the unauthorized removal of the documents with respect to their admissibility in court?
A: The court held that the unauthorized removal of the documents did not bar their admissibility. The court emphasized that the test of admissibility of evidence lies in its relevancy, and documents in the public domain should not be excluded merely due to the manner in which they were obtained.
Case: Yashwant Sinha & Ors. vs. Central Bureau of Investigation
Date: 10th April 2019

---

Q: What was the court's stance on the claim of privilege under Section 123 of the Indian Evidence Act?
A: The court found the claim of privilege under Section 123 of the Indian Evidence Act to be untenable, especially since the documents were already in the public domain and accessible to the entire citizenry.
Case: Yashwant Sinha & Ors. vs. Central Bureau of Investigation
Date: 10th April 2019

---

Q: How did the court reconcile the Right to Information Act with the Official Secrets Act in this case?
A: The court noted that the Right to Information Act allows access to information if public interest in disclosure outweighs the harm to protected interests, even when considering the Official Secrets Act. The court emphasized that transparency and accountability are vital in curbing corruption.
Case: Yashwant Sinha & Ors. vs. Central Bureau of Investigation
Date: 10th April 2019

---

Q: What did the court state about the relevance of public interest in the context of the Right to Information Act?
A: The court highlighted that the Right to Information Act serves the public interest by promoting transparency and accountability. It allows for the disclosure of information if public interest outweighs the harm, even when such information is protected under laws like the Official Secrets Act.
Case: Yashwant Sinha & Ors. vs. Central Bureau of Investigation
Date: 10th April 2019

---

Q: How did the court address the argument about the documents being improperly obtained?
A: The court addressed this argument by stating that even if the documents were improperly obtained, their relevancy and public availability justified their consideration. The court emphasized that the manner of obtaining evidence does not preclude its admissibility if it is relevant and in the public interest.
Case: Yashwant Sinha & Ors. vs. Central Bureau of Investigation
Date: 10th April 2019

---

Q: Who were the respondents in the OLX India B.V. vs. State of Haryana & Ors. case?

Case: OLX India B.V. vs. State of Haryana & Ors.
Date: 8th March 2022

---

Q: What is the significance of this judgment for OLX India B.V. and similar internet platforms?
A: The judgment signifies that internet platforms like OLX India B.V. are not liable to perform extensive verifications and guarantee the genuineness of deals, reinforcing their role as intermediaries rather than guarantors of the transactions facilitated on their platforms.
Case: OLX India B.V. vs. State of Haryana & Ors.

Case: Abu Salem Abdul Kayyum Ansari vs. The State of Maharashtra

Q: What was the primary legal issue in the case of Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.?
A: The primary legal issue was whether the appellant could be prosecuted under the Prevention of Food Adulteration Act (PFA) for misbranding after the Food Safety and Standards Act (FSSA) had come into force.
Case: Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.
Date: 14th December 2023

---

Q: What were the dates of the relevant provisions of the Food Safety and Standards Act (FSSA) coming into force in the case of Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.?
A: The definition of 'misbranded food' under Section 3 of the FSSA came into force on 28th May 2008, and Section 52 of the FSSA, which provides for penalties for misbranding, was brought into force on 29th July 2010.
Case: Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.
Date: 14th December 2023

---

Q: What was the appellant's position in the company involved in the case of Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.?
A: The appellant, Manik Hiru Jhangiani, was a Director of M/s. Bharti Retail Limited, a company operating retail stores under the name 'Easy Day'.
Case: Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.
Date: 14th December 2023

---

Q: What argument did the appellant make regarding the applicability of Section 52 of the FSSA in the case of Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.?
A: The appellant argued that after 29th July 2010, the provisions of the FSSA, including Section 52 which provides for penalties for misbranding, would prevail over the PFA, and thus the prosecution under the PFA was not valid.
Case: Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.
Date: 14th December 2023

---

Q: How might the ruling in the case of Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P. impact future prosecutions under the PFA?
A: Future prosecutions under the PFA may be affected in that if an inconsistency arises with the FSSA, the provisions of the FSSA will prevail, potentially limiting the applicability of the PFA.
Case: Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.
Date: 14th December 2023

---

Q: How does Section 89 of the FSSA influence the outcome of cases involving both the FSSA and the PFA as seen in the case of Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.?
A: Section 89 of the FSSA ensures that the FSSA's provisions will override those of the PFA in the event of any inconsistency, thereby influencing courts to apply the FSSA's penalties and provisions in such cases.
Case: Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.
Date: 14th December 2023

---

Q: What procedural steps did the Food Inspector take after finding the misbranded food in the case of Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.?
A: The Food Inspector purchased the biscuit packets, drew a panchnama the next day, and sent samples to the State Food Laboratory, Bhopal, for analysis and testing.
Case: Manik Hiru Jhangiani vs State of M.P.
Date: 14th December 2023

Q: What could be the implications of this judgment for Haribhau?
A: Haribhau is no longer required to serve additional jail time beyond the one month already served, provided he pays the enhanced fine of Rs. 15,000 within the specified period.
Case: Haribhau vs The State of Maharashtra

Q: What was the main issue in the case of S. Athilakshmi vs The State Rep. by The Drugs Inspector?
A: The main issue was whether S. Athilakshmi, a registered medical practitioner, violated the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940, by stocking medicines for sale without a valid drug license.
Case: S. Athilakshmi vs The State Rep. by The Drugs Inspector
Date: 15th March 2023

---

Q: What did the Drugs Inspector find during the inspection of S. Athilakshmi's premises?
A: During the inspection on 16th March 2016, the Drugs Inspector found various medicines in the inner room of her premises, including lotions and ointments.
Case: S. Athilakshmi vs The State Rep. by The Drugs Inspector
Date: 15th March 2023

---

Q: What legal provisions were cited for the prosecution of S. Athilakshmi?
A: The prosecution was based on Section 18(c) of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940, for stocking drugs for sale without a valid license, which is punishable under Section 27(b)(ii) of the Act.
Case: S. Athilakshmi vs The State Rep. by The Drugs Inspector
Date: 15th March 2023

---

Q: What was the appellant's argument regarding the quantity and purpose of the medicines found?
A: The appellant argued that the small quantity of medicines found, mostly lotions and ointments, were for emergency use by her patients and not for sale, as protected under Schedule K of the Drugs and Cosmetics Rules, 1945.
Case: S. Athilakshmi vs The State Rep. by The Drugs Inspector
Date: 15th March 2023

---

Q: What is the significance of Schedule K in this case?
A: Schedule K of the Drugs and Cosmetics Rules, 1945, provides exemptions for drugs supplied by a registered medical practitioner to their patients, which was relevant in protecting the appellant from prosecution.
Case: S. Athilakshmi vs The State Rep. by The Drugs Inspector
Date: 15th March 2023

---

Q: What could be the implications of this judgment for other medical practitioners?
A: This judgment may provide relief to other registered medical practitioners who stock small quantities of medicines for emergency use by their patients, ensuring they are protected under Schedule K.
Case: S. Athilakshmi vs The State Rep. by The Drugs Inspector
Date: 15th March 2023

---

Case: MD. ANOWAR HUSSAIN vs. STATE OF ASSAM
Date: 13th October 2022

---

Q: What was the significance of the post-mortem report in this case?
A: The post-mortem report was significant as it established that the cause of death was asphyxia due to throttling, which contradicted the defense's claim of death due to illness.

Q: What was the defense's argument regarding the tenancy agreement presented by the complainant?
A: The defense argued that the tenancy agreement presented by the complainant was forged, as it included the Indian Rupee symbol which did not exist at the time the agreement was supposedly made.
Case: Vishnu Kumar Shukla & Anr. vs. The State of Uttar Pradesh & Anr.

Q: What was the decision of the Chief Judicial Magistrate, Lucknow regarding the application for discharge filed by the appellants?
A: The Chief Judicial Magistrate, Lucknow rejected the application for discharge filed by the appellants.
Case: Vishnu Kumar Shukla & Anr. vs. The State of Uttar Pradesh & Anr.

---

Q: How might this judgment affect future cases involving allegations based on forged documents?
A: This judgment sets a precedent that courts must thoroughly scrutinize allegations based on documentary evidence and protect individuals from prosecution based on forged or fabricated documents.
Case: Vishnu Kumar Shukla & Anr. vs. The State of Uttar Pradesh & Anr.

---

Q: What could be the potential implications for the complainant, Ram Kumar Garg, following this judgment?
A: The complainant, Ram Kumar Garg, could face legal consequences for perjury and forgery if further proceedings find that he knowingly filed a false complaint based on fabricated documents.
Case: Vishnu Kumar Shukla & Anr. vs. The State of Uttar Pradesh & Anr.

Q: What might be the implications of this judgment for future cases involving long-pending offenses?
A: This judgment suggests that in cases involving long-pending offenses, especially those where a significant amount of time has elapsed, courts may consider probation instead of imprisonment, emphasizing rehabilitation over punishment.
Case: Tarak Nath Keshari vs. State of West Bengal

Q: What was the main issue in the case of M/S Shree Daneshwari Traders vs. Sanjay Jain & Anr.?
A: The main issue was whether Sanjay Jain was guilty under Section 138 of the Negotiable Instruments Act for issuing cheques that were dishonored due to insufficient funds.
Case: M/S Shree Daneshwari Traders vs. Sanjay Jain & Anr.

---

Q: What were the allegations against Sanjay Jain?
A: Sanjay Jain was alleged to have issued several cheques to M/S Shree Daneshwari Traders as payment for commodities supplied, which were dishonored upon presentation due to insufficient funds.
Case: M/S Shree Daneshwari Traders vs. Sanjay Jain & Anr.

---

Q: How did the respondent Sanjay Jain respond to the dishonored cheques?
A: Sanjay Jain acknowledged the receipt of legal notices regarding the dishonored cheques but failed to make the payments or arrange for the amounts in satisfaction of the cheques issued.
Case: M/S Shree Daneshwari Traders vs. Sanjay Jain & Anr.

---

Q: What was the decision of the Trial Court regarding Sanjay Jain's case?
A: The Trial Court acquitted Sanjay Jain, stating that the cheques were issued as security for credit purchases and not for the discharge of a legally enforceable debt.
Case: M/S Shree Daneshwari Traders vs. Sanjay Jain & Anr.

---

Q: What impact does the judgement in State of Maharashtra vs. Surendra Pundalik Gadling & Ors. have on future cases of a similar nature?
A: The judgement sets a precedent for strict interpretation and application of the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967, in cases involving alleged conspiracies and acts of violence.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Surendra Pundalik Gadling & Ors.

---

Q: How did the court address the defense's claim of fabricated evidence in the State of Maharashtra vs. Surendra Pundalik Gadling & Ors. case?
A: The court examined the evidence and found it credible and consistent, rejecting the defense's claim of fabrication based on the corroborative material presented by the prosecution.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Surendra Pundalik Gadling & Ors.

---

Q: What clarifications did the court provide regarding the application of the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967, in this case?
A: The court clarified that the act's provisions were applicable in cases where there is substantial evidence of planning and executing unlawful activities, even if direct evidence of participation in every act is not available.
Case: State of Maharashtra vs. Surendra Pundalik Gadling & Ors.

---

Q: How might this judgment influence the treatment of hostile witnesses in future trials?
A: This judgment clarifies that hostile witness testimonies, if credible in parts, can still be used to support the prosecution's case, encouraging courts to carefully scrutinize such evidence rather than dismissing it entirely.
Case: Selvamani vs. State Rep. by the Inspector of Police

---

Q: What was the appellant's second argument regarding the remand orders?
A: The appellant argued that the remand orders were issued without proper consideration of the statutory requirements and procedural safeguards, particularly the conditions under which remand can be granted under the Prevention of Money Laundering Act, 2002.
Case: V. Senthil Balaji v. The State Represented by Deputy Director and Ors.
